# Provenance of the handing-over-to-readers rules

One line per shipped rule of `references/rules.md`, keyed `R-n` by the operator's number, one per bullet of its "What the research adds", and one per clause of `references/explaining.md`, `references/explainer.md`, `references/media.md` and `references/page.md`, keyed `Explaining:`, `Explainer:`, `Media:` and `Page:`; the lines keyed `Intake:`, `Gate:` and `Feedback:` cover `references/intake.md`, the ledger with its gate and the usage journal, and the line keyed `Evidence:` a clause of `references/evidence.md`. The lines on the explanation page cite the research note `research/2026-11-14_explanation-page-evidence.md` by section, and the lines that entered with the review of that page by four of its readers cite `research/2026-11-22_explainer-review-evidence.md`. The clause lines cite the research note `research/2026-11-07_report-forms-evidence.md` by section: § The readings holds what the operator said at each of his seven readings of report forms (Reading 1 … Reading 7), § Measurements the counts taken on the texts, § What the literature adds the opened sources. Each line holds the sources the rule or clause was drawn from with their locators, its formulation and its caveat, then the tests and documents found for it, then its record in the format of `../../scientific-method/references/record-format.md`. A line whose claim waits for its test is its owner's decision, and its record names the claim as `H-<id of the line>` in `pending-evidence.md`. `citations.json` holds the words and figures the lines quote from their sources, one entry per quote. A source is `first-hand` when its text (abstract, HTML or full text, as the pointer says) was opened; a figure seen only in a search summary is `relay` and carries no rule. `README n` is entry n of the repository `README.md` §References, verified first-hand by the output-style programme. The formulation of every rule is the operator's list of 27 rules; a second formulation is named where one exists.

- **R-1** · Rao & Daumé III 2018, *Learning to Ask Good Questions*, arXiv:1805.04655 (PDF: "a good question is one whose expected answer will be useful") · the operator's list · eval client-question-list E3–E7 · the paper values a question by the use of its answer; it does not test the definition of a candidate · Rao & Daumé III Learning to Ask Good Questions arXiv:1805.04655 §5.2, Table 2 — a model that ranks the ten clarification questions retrieved for a StackExchange post by the expected utility of their answers picked the question experts marked best more often than neural baselines (p@1 27.7 against 25.2 for the strongest), a test of ranking candidates already retrieved, not of where candidates come from; Vijayvargiya, Viswanathan & Neubig Asking What Matters: Reward-Driven Clarification arXiv:2604.14624 §6.4, Table 3 — on 500 underspecified SWE-bench Verified issues with a simulated user, an 8B question module trained to ask for information the user can supply and that bears on success resolved 36.8% with 3.0 questions against 35.6% with 5.1 for GPT-5, the answerability of the two sets alike (.373 against .369) and the source naming no test; Jiang, Pahwa, Zhang, Zhang, Xia & Soatto Learning When Human Decisions Matter arXiv:2611.05814 §1 — holds that humans supply private context, authority or judgments unavailable to the agent, and takes the candidate consultation points as already identified, its tests comparing ways of spending a budget of questions among them; Grunde-McLaughlin et al. Agentic Path Maps arXiv:2611.04183 §6.1, Table 2 — 48 novice analysts reviewing a data-analysis agent's result put less weight on an extreme output when shown three or nine analyses of the question than when shown one analysis, picked for its minimum or maximum output to isolate anchoring (effect -0.63 [-0.99, -0.24], posterior probability 0.9992 in a Bayesian linear mixed model), at a higher intrinsic cognitive load (0.40 [0.10, 0.70]) and a higher extraneous cognitive load (0.31 [-0.06, 0.67]), under other conditions: novice analysts estimating a relationship from the analyses shown to them, not an addressee answering an agent's questions or weighing the decisions it took itself · kind: decision · owner: the repository owner · goal: the question list holds only the points where the work needs the addressee's decision or data he alone holds, and the agent's own choices are taken and recorded rather than asked · hypothesis: H-R-1

Mayer, Heiser & Lonn had 78 college students watch a narrated animation of how lightning forms with or without six additional sentences of entertaining but conceptually irrelevant information, and with them retention was 3.76 against 5.07 idea units (F(1,74) = 14.39, p < .001) and transfer 3.05 against 3.95 solutions (F(1, 74) = 5.67, p < .05), effect sizes .56 and .55, in the claim's direction under other conditions: a system-paced lesson, and added sentences chosen to interest the learner rather than surplus of the matter; Mayer, Bove, Bryman, Mars & Tapangco report from three experiments with college students that a summary of short captions with simple illustrations was recalled and solved transfer problems from as well as or better than the full text with or without the summary, and that adding text to the summary reduced its effectiveness, the abstract giving no figure and naming no test, in the claim's direction under other conditions: a science lesson, and captions with illustrations in place of prose; Rey meta-analysed 39 experimental effects and reports a significant seductive detail effect, small to medium on retention and medium on transfer, the abstract giving no figure and naming no test, in the claim's direction under other conditions: interesting but irrelevant details in instructional messages; Sundararajan & Adesope report that including seductive details in learning material can hinder learning, with findings mixed on the degree and the effect moderated by design and method, the abstract giving no figure and naming no test, in the claim's direction under the same other conditions; Charney, Reder & Wells gave 40 inexperienced computer users an elaborated manual of 11,000 words or an unelaborated one of 3,500 words to read for forty-five minutes before file tasks done without it, 20 of them told the tasks in advance and 20 not, half of each group with each manual, and both manuals gave about the same share of tasks completed, .80 against .80 among those told and .85 against .76 among those not told; those not told took 29.4 against 40.2 minutes and 76.8 against 101.8 commands with the elaborated manual, and those told took 33.5 against 36.1 minutes and 95.8 against 94.2 commands, which the authors read as much more efficient with the unelaborated manual, the chapter naming no test and referring to Reder, Charney & Morgan 1986 for the study; in Study 2, 40 novice and 40 experienced users, none told the tasks, read one of four versions crossing rich or sparse procedural with rich or sparse conceptual elaborations, and with rich procedural elaborations, examples of correct commands and details of notational conventions, they took 37.4 and 37.7 minutes and 71.7 and 73.7 commands, with rich and sparse conceptual elaborations, against 43.5 and 45.9 minutes and 88.7 and 92.4 commands with sparse procedural ones, called significantly worse with no test named, while conceptual elaborations had no effect, so that cutting the conceptual elaborations cost nothing and cutting the procedural ones cost time and commands, under other conditions: time and commands for a skill applied from a manual taken away before the tasks in place of the points of a report recalled, elaborations the reader used to apply the commands in place of sentences that answer none of his questions, and the effect of length bounded by whether the reader knows his task in advance; Steyvers et al. had participants judge the probability that an LLM's answer to a multiple-choice or short-answer question was correct, and in Experiments 2a to 2c, of 60, 60 and 59 participants, long explanations of median 115, 64 and 95 words raised their confidence above short explanations of median 34, 24 and 24 words (BF = 25, the three experiments combined), while the mean participant AUC was 0.54 with the long explanations and 0.57 with uncertainty-only responses of median 9 words, which kept the answer and its expression of uncertainty and dropped the rationale (BF = .23, moderate evidence for no difference on the source's scale), the longer text giving no better discrimination than the answer with its uncertainty alone, and §3.1 giving no AUC for the short explanations, under other conditions: quiz answers judged for correctness in place of a report, confidence and telling right answers from wrong in place of retelling, long explanations compared with responses stripped of their rationale and not with a text cut to the sentences the reader needs, and a Bayes factor from Bayesian t-tests in place of a test against chance; doi:10.1016/j.edurev.2012.05.003 and doi:10.1007/s10648-020-09522-4: experiments on seductive details in instructional messages, meta-analysed in 2012 and 2020; Charney, Reder & Wells: in Study 1, 40 inexperienced computer users, 20 told the file tasks in advance and 20 not, 10 per manual in each group, with PC-DOS manuals of 11,000 and 3,500 words read for forty-five minutes and taken away before the tasks, and in Study 2, 40 novice and 40 experienced computer users who had never used an IBM-PC, none told the tasks, with four versions of that manual, in a chapter of 1988 reporting studies of Reder, Charney & Morgan 1986; the two tests that told their effect from chance fixed the study time and measured recall for a memory test, so whether the reader reads a short report to its end or sooner is untested; the summaries ran to 800 and 1,000 words and the cut lesson was a narration, so the size of twenty to thirty sentences and about two hundred words is untested and rests on the operator's readings; Charney, Reder & Wells found the unelaborated manual worse for readers who did not know their tasks in advance and, by the authors' reading, more efficient for those who did, and the manuals cut of their examples of correct commands worse for readers not told the tasks, with no test named; Steyvers et al. compared long explanations with responses stripped of their rationale, not with a text cut to what the reader needs; and Friedman et al. found the shortest outputs worst at catching a wrong conclusion

- **Explaining: the cold read, what it shows and what it does not** · the measurement `evals/cold-read/`: four reports of `evals/runs/iteration-2` written with the skill, each with three planted omissions (the reader's decision, the cause of the problem, a sentence that introduces a thing), read blind by a fresh agent, one run a cell. `runs/iteration-3` (the shipped instructions, six questions): the decision marked in 4 of 4, the cause in 4 of 4, the introducing sentence in 0 of 4, nothing marked on the 4 intact reports. `runs/iteration-2` (the same questions, the instructions before the context audit's wording fixes): the same outcome in all sixteen cells. `runs/iteration-1` (the first prompt, five questions): 2 of 4 intact reports marked on a question, and 3 to 6 terms listed as unexplained on every intact report, which is why the terms change no exit code and the prompt names what is left out. `runs/iteration-4` and `runs/iteration-5` (the shipped instructions read by two other models): the decision and the cause in 4 of 4, the introducing sentence in 1 of 4, and one intact report marked on the question about what is unverified by each, which is why a mark is weighed against the text and not applied as a defect. `runs/iteration-6` (the instructions that add the retelling of a first part, the same six questions without a request to retell, read by Opus): the decision marked in 4 of 4, the cause in 4 of 4, the introducing sentence in 0 of 4, nothing marked on the 4 intact reports, the outcome of `runs/iteration-3` in all sixteen cells · a hands-on measurement on real reports (`research/2026-11-07_report-forms-evidence.md` § The cold read on real reports): 60 closing messages of one operator's sessions, 30 he asked back about and 30 he approved, one read each against the six default questions; a question left open in 30 and 28 of them, 3.43 against 2.43 a report; of the 30 things he asked back about the read named 8, touched 11 and concerned none of 11; the reports predate the story form and got all six questions, where the skill puts the questions of the report's own registry · Lentz & de Jong 1997 (line "Explaining: the reader's questions come first"): experts predict few of a reader's problems, which says nothing of a model reading in his place · the session's design: questions without the author's answers, an answer counted only with a quoted sentence that stands in the report; a second read only after a fix that changes a whole part is a house limit on cost, not measured · two runs a cell under near-identical instructions on one reader model and one run a cell on each of two others, four reports of one size, omissions planted by the session; Joseph et al. 2026, *Generating Edit-Inducing Questions for AI Research Manuscripts*, arXiv:2609.36617 §4 and Table 2 — GPT-4o and o3 reading 116 paper drafts asked about 140 to 206 questions a paper, of which 7-10 % were answered only in the final version, against 38 % of the reviewers' three questions a paper;
